invariant), `docs/arcs/vocabulary-arc.md:80` (`vocabulary/F3`, which owns `block-id`'s reach), `.planning/DISPLAY-LAYER-GAP.md:296` (D10), `records/author-calls.md:411-419` (the fifth item, the fork this row serves). Two external fetches failed and are named: `raw.githubusercontent.com/ProseMirror/prosemirror-docs/master/src/content/docs/guide/index.md` returned HTTP 404 and the ProseMirror claims here are pinned from the two library sources instead; `unison-lang.org/docs/the-big-idea/` returned HTTP 200 and was rejected as a pin because the served page wraps every word in its own span, which makes a line-addressed quote unreadable, so the Unison claims are pinned from the repository's own README, repository-format document and design note. `ledger-lint` measured after this row at the figure in the report.
- checked:  2026-09-15
- element:  `none`. This row surveys and prices; it designs nothing, opens no roster row, and answers no author call. It leaves the ruling in the author's hands and narrows it to two supported shapes. Either `d-tag` gains a second field beside a closed `Role`, which is ProseMirror's and Peritext's arrangement and keeps shard G inside `Doc` at zero width, or shard G leaves `Doc` for an object beside it, which is Emacs's overlay arrangement and costs the property that an address rides on rendered output. The record rules out the third shape, an address-bearing arm of `Role` resolved through `lookup-face`, on the strength of HTMLDOM:2028 and of fifteen mechanisms none of which shares one field.

### FD-32 ten of fourteen surveyed projects carry an expected-failure mechanism, two of them read the cause with a tool that changes the verdict, exactly one splits expected-failure into two result codes by whether the cause is the project's own bug or the environment's, and no surveyed project states any rule about what fraction of a suite may be expected-fail

- state:    OPEN
- claim:    The question, aimed at the W6 gate proposal over `prog/demo/` and at the argument that a **stated** failure differs from an **unanswered** one: how do test harnesses represent a test expected to fail, and does any of them distinguish a failure with a known stated cause from a failure whose cause is undecided? **Fourteen projects were read at their own sources: pytest, LLVM lit, TAP 14, KTAP, kselftest, Chromium web tests, web-platform-tests `wptrunner`, Automake, DejaGnu, the GDB testsuite, Rust, Go, Jest and CTest. Ten of the fourteen carry an expected-failure mechanism. The answer has five parts. (a) Three mechanisms are distinct and every surveyed project offers one or two of them: skip, where the subject does not run; expected failure, where it runs and its failure is tolerated; strict expected failure, where an unexpected pass is itself a failure. A fourth shape, inverted assertion, is offered by CTest, Jest and Rust and is a different thing: failing IS the pass criterion, so the expectation cannot record a defect. (b) Twelve of the fourteen offer somewhere to put a cause and eight of those twelve have no tool that reads it. The four that read a cause read a machine-evaluable one, in three different ways: pytest's `raises` and `condition` and lit's `XFAIL:` target expression change the verdict, `wptrunner`'s `implementation-status` enumeration decides whether the subject runs at all, and Chromium's bug identifier is checked for presence by a linter. Jest and CTest offer no cause field at all. A free-text `reason` is documentary in every system that has one, and DejaGnu says so in its own manual. (c) The stated-against-undecided distinction is formalized in exactly one surveyed place. The GDB testsuite splits it into two result codes: `KFAIL` for a known problem of GDB itself, where a bug number is mandatory, and `XFAIL` for a known problem of the environment, where a bug number is requested only if one exists. No tool acts on the split; it is a naming and review discipline. (d) No surveyed project states any rule or guidance about what proportion of a suite may be expected-fail. Chromium's is the largest expectation file in the survey at 9,205 lines and it states rules about mechanism choice, bug identifiers and duplicates, and none about proportion. (e) No project that has strict expected failure treats strictness as sufficient on its own. Every one of them adds a second control, and every implementation narrows the tolerance so it cannot swallow an arbitrary failure mode.**
- measured: **1. THE THREE MECHANISMS, ASSIGNED, AND A FOURTH SHAPE THAT IS NONE OF THEM.** *Skip only*: Go, Rust. *Skip and expected failure*: TAP 14, KTAP, kselftest, Automake. *Skip, expected failure, and strict expected failure*: pytest. *Expected failure that is always strict*: LLVM lit, DejaGnu, Chromium, `wptrunner`, the GDB testsuite. *Inverted assertion*: CTest `WILL_FAIL`, Jest `test.failing`, Rust `should_panic`. The fourth is a different object and this survey keeps it apart. CTESTWILLFAIL:4 "If ``true``, inverts the pass / fail test criteria. Tests for which" `WILL_FAIL` is true fail with return code 0, and JESTAPI:761 "Use `test.failing` when you are writing a test and expecting it to fail. These tests will behave the other way normal tests do. If `failing` test will throw any errors then it will pass. If it does not throw it will fail." **Under inverted assertion the failure is the pass criterion, so the record carries no statement that anything is broken, and strictness costs nothing because there is no tolerance to be strict about.** Jest names the use it was built for, JESTAPI:767 "It can also be a nice way to contribute failing tests to a project, even if you don't know how to fix the bug." **2. PYTEST: ALL THREE MECHANISMS, A DOCUMENTARY REASON, AND A CAUSE THAT CHANGES THE VERDICT.** Two of the three are defined against each other, PYTESTSKIP:17 "An **xfail** means that you expect a test to fail for some reason." where a skip does not run the subject at all, and the third is named, PYTESTSKIP:20 "it's an **xpass** and will be reported in the test summary." The marker carries five knobs, PYTESTREF:269 "pytest.mark.xfail(condition=True, *, reason=None, raises=None, run=True, strict=strict_xfail)". **The runtime reads four of the five and leaves `reason` alone.** `reason` reaches two assignments in the report hook, both of them report text: PYTESTSRC:302 and :311 set `rep.wasxfail` from it, and PYTESTSRC:308 prefixes it into `rep.longrepr` under a strict unexpected pass. `raises` decides the verdict. PYTESTSRC:290 "            raises = xfailed.raises" gates the outcome assigned at :301 against the plain failure assigned at :304, and the documentation states the consequence, PYTESTSKIP:301 "exception not mentioned in ``raises``." is reported as a regular failure, with PYTESTREF:278 "Exception class (or tuple of classes) expected to be raised by the test function; other exceptions will fail the test." **So pytest is the one surveyed harness where a stated cause narrows the tolerance: an expectation naming `ValueError` does not absorb an error of another shape.** `condition` is read the same way, and one form of `reason` is mandatory, PYTESTSRC:155 "you need to specify reason=STRING when using booleans as conditions." **That requirement reaches only the explicit-boolean form.** The stored record has four fields, PYTESTSRC:201 "    __slots__ = (" over `raises`, `reason`, `run` and `strict`, and the unconditional branch at :231 defaults `reason` to the empty string, so a bare expectation carrying no cause at all is legal. Strictness is opt-in, PYTESTSKIP:322 "Both ``XFAIL`` and ``XPASS`` don't fail the test suite by default." against PYTESTSKIP:331 "results from this test to fail the test suite." once `strict=True` is set. A run-nothing form exists for a subject that crashes the interpreter, PYTESTSKIP:307 "be executed, use the ``run`` parameter as ``False``:". **3. LLVM LIT: STRICT WITH NO SWITCH, THE CAUSE IS THE CONDITION, AND NO REASON FIELD EXISTS.** The directive is defined by its inversion, LITGUIDE:626 "An `XFAIL` test will be successful if its execution fails, and" a failure if it succeeds, and the condition is a boolean feature expression, LITGUIDE:654 "- `XFAIL` expects the test to fail if any expression is true." **The condition is the whole cause and lit stores nothing beside it.** An expectation whose cause nobody has narrowed has its own spelling, LITGUIDE:656 "Use, `XFAIL: *` if the test is expected to fail everywhere. Similarly, use" `UNSUPPORTED` to disable it everywhere. Strictness is in the result table with no option, LITTESTPY:52 "XFAIL = ResultCode(" carries `isFailure` false and LITTESTPY:57 "XPASS = ResultCode(" carries it true, and the guide states it, LITCMD:512 "unexpectedly passed (**XPASS**) tests are considered failures." **The conversion is narrow and the narrowing is load-bearing.** Only a plain failure is absorbed, LITTESTPY:312 "                elif result.code is FAIL:" is the sole branch that yields `XFAIL`, which leaves `UNRESOLVED` and `TIMEOUT` red on an expected-failing subject. Lit's stated reason for preferring `XFAIL` over `UNSUPPORTED` is the tree's own `NEWPASS` argument, LITGUIDE:684 "but expects a failure output, taking extra execution time but alerting you" if and when the test begins to behave correctly. **An escape hatch weakens the strictness and the guide states its purpose**, LITCMD:367 "primary purpose is to suppress an `XPASS` result without modifying a test" case that uses the `XFAIL` directive. **4. TAP 14 AND KTAP: THE TWO SPECIFICATIONS, AND THE ONE THAT FORBIDS A STRICT READING.** TAP puts the cause in the grammar as an optional trailing field, TAP14:64 "Directive   := " followed by a todo-or-skip keyword and an optional reason, with two directives defined, TAP14:360 "currently defined: `TODO` and `SKIP`." The field is named an explanation for both, TAP14:473 "and any text after `TODO\S*\s+` is the explanation." and TAP14:496 "test, and the text after `SKIP\S*\s+` is the explanation." and the two are separated by whether the subject ran, TAP14:480 "These tests represent a feature to be implemented or a bug to be fixed and" carry no expectation of success, against TAP14:503 "These tests indicate that a test was not run, or if it was, that its" success or failure is being temporarily ignored. **TAP forbids treating a failing `TODO` as a failure and makes the unexpected-pass report optional.** The prohibition is a MUST NOT, TAP14:488 "Harnesses _must not_ treat failing `TODO` test points as a test failure." and the unexpected pass is a MAY, TAP14:484 "Should a todo test point begin succeeding, the harness _may_ report it in" some way. **So the one specification in the survey that states a rule here states the opposite of strictness, and offers no strict mode at all.** KTAP adds `XFAIL` beside the inherited `TODO` and states a preference between the two, KTAPSPEC:101 ", which indicates that a test is not expected to pass at the moment," carrying KTAPSPEC:103 "  directive is inherited from TAP, its use in the kernel is discouraged." against KTAPSPEC:104 "which indicates that a test is expected to fail. This is similar" to `TODO`. **Neither specification attaches machine-readable structure to a cause. The field is prose in both.** **5. KSELFTEST: SIX RESULT CODES, AND AN UNEXPECTED PASS THAT COUNTS AS A PASS.** The vocabulary is six wide and one line prints all six, KSELFTESTH:170 "# Totals: pass:%u fail:%u xfail:%u xpass:%u skip:%u error:%u". **Both directions emit the TAP success token.** KSELFTESTH:253 "ok %u # XFAIL " and KSELFTESTH:267 "ok %u # XPASS ". The exit decision folds the unexpected pass into the satisfied plan, KSELFTESTH:397 " * ksft_finished() - Exit selftest with success if all tests passed" summing the pass count with KSELFTESTH:402 "		  ksft_cnt.ksft_xpass +" and the xfail and skip counts. **So a kselftest subject that starts passing leaves the run green, and the kernel's own harness is the survey's clearest instance of a non-strict expected failure.** The message argument to each emitter is a `printf` format written into the diagnostic and read by nothing. **6. CHROMIUM: THE ONE SURVEYED PLACE A CAUSE IS MECHANICALLY REQUIRED, AND THREE FILES SPLIT BY KIND OF CAUSE.** The requirement is stated and it names its enforcement, CHROMEXPDOC:277 "Lines are expected to have one or more bug identifiers, and the linter will" CHROMEXPDOC:278 "  complain about lines missing them. Bug identifiers are of the form" `crbug.com/12345` and two other accepted spellings. **Measured against the live file: 6,793 expectation lines, 6,369 carrying a `crbug` reference, and 504 carrying none of the three accepted forms.** The linter complains and does not refuse, which those 504 lines demonstrate; that reading is inference from the count beside the word complain, and no source pinned here states the linter's exit behaviour. **The expectation is an exact outcome set, so a pass is an unexpected result, and the doc says why that matters**, CHROMEXPDOC:306 "that the test will still be run, so that we can notice if it doesn't actually" crash. A tolerated-flakiness form is spelled as a list, CHROMEXPDOC:294 "  If multiple expectations are listed, the test is considered" flaky and any of those results is expected. **The kind of cause selects the file, and that is the closest external analogue of the tree's track-deferral-against-blocking-condition split.** A temporary suppression goes to `TestExpectations`, CHROMEXPDOC:210 "  main test failure suppression file. In theory, this should be used for" temporarily marking tests as flaky. A permanent material fact goes to `NeverFixTests`, CHROMEXPDOC:52 "test is never supposed to pass (e.g. it's testing Windows-specific behavior, so" can't ever pass on Linux/Mac), and that file closes the admissible cause set, CHROMNEVERFIX:15 "# This file should only include tests that we never want to run." with CHROMNEVERFIX:16 "# There should only be the following categories of tests here:" followed by eight numbered categories. A suppression that has aged goes to `StaleTestExpectations`, CHROMEXPDOC:231 "  They're moved here to get them out of the way of people doing rebaselines" since they're clearly not getting fixed anytime soon. **The main file states a rule preferring an expectation over a skip, and its stated reason is the tree's own `NEWPASS` argument**, CHROMTESTEXP:9 "# Single [ Skip ] expectation is not allowed in this file. Normally we should" CHROMTESTEXP:10 "# not skip a test because it's failing or flaky. We should add failure or flaky" CHROMTESTEXP:11 "# expectations instead, so that they will still run on bots, and we can collect" data about their flakiness. One further mechanical rule exists and it is about duplication, CHROMEXPDOC:369 "Duplicate expectations are not allowed within the file and will generate" warnings. **7. WEB-PLATFORM-TESTS: A MACHINE-READABLE CAUSE ENUMERATION, AND A FLAG FOR THE ALL-UNEXPECTED-PASSES CASE.** The expectation is an exact outcome or an ordered list, WPTMETA:126 "      typical expected test outcome, and subsequent values indicate" known intermittent outcomes, and it can be defaulted for a whole file, WPTMETA:190 "   example, a top level key with ``expected: FAIL`` would indicate" that all tests and subtests in the file are expected to fail. **`wptrunner` carries the survey's only enumerated cause field, and a command-line switch acts on it.** WPTMETA:155 "     One of the values ``implementing``," `not-implementing` or `backlog`, used WPTMETA:157 "     with the ``--skip-implementation-status`` command line argument to" WPTMETA:158 "     ``wptrunner`` to ignore certain features where running the test is" low value. **Three values, and they are three states of an answer: in progress, settled as refused, and deferred with no date.** A tool reads the field and what it does is drop the subject from the run, so the field never changes how a failure is scored. The only bug link in the format rides on the `disabled` key as its value, WPTMETA:209 "    disabled: http://test.bugs.example.org/bugs/12345". **Strictness is on by default, and the opt-out has its own flag for the case where every unexpected result was a pass.** WPTRUNNER:396 "    if test_status.unexpected and not kwargs[" opens the general tolerance, WPTRUNNER:403 "Tolerating {test_status.unexpected_pass} unexpected results" covers the narrower one, and the default verdict is WPTRUNNER:407 "    return test_status.unexpected == 0". Both switches are declared in negative form, WPTCLI:81 "--no-fail-on-unexpected-pass" with `action="store_false"` and WPTCLI:83 "Exit with status code 0 when all unexpected results are PASS", so both default true. **8. AUTOMAKE: GUIDANCE TO STATE THE CAUSE, AND A MANUAL THAT SAYS THE FIELD IS IGNORED.** The manual gives the survey's plainest guidance on writing a cause down, AUTOMAKE:9719 "failure} (or @emph{xfail}).  When writing such a test, it is highly" AUTOMAKE:9720 "desirable to note in the test what has to happen to make the test" AUTOMAKE:9721 "succeed.  In case a test that is expected to fail ends up passing" instead, many testing environments flag it as an unexpected pass. **That guidance names no field to hold it, and the field that does exist is documented as discarded.** The mechanism is a name list in the makefile, AUTOMAKE:9785 "(usually a subset of @code{TESTS}) that are expected to fail; this will" AUTOMAKE:9786 "effectively reverse the result of those tests (with the provision that" skips and hard errors remain untouched, and the per-result annotation is thrown away, AUTOMAKE:10537 "test; the harness will ignore such extra text when generating" `test-suite.log` and preparing the testsuite summary. **An unexpected pass is grouped with failures twice in the manual.** AUTOMAKE:10057 "succeeded unexpectedly, or---unless the variable @code{IGNORE_SKIPPED_LOGS}" is set, collects that output into the summary log beside failures and hard errors, and AUTOMAKE:10232 "You can ensure that all tests are rerun which have failed or passed" unexpectedly by running `make recheck`. The driver protocol exposes the expectation as a boolean with no cause slot, AUTOMAKE:10475 "Whether the tested program is expected to fail." **The tree's own `KNOWN_FAIL` has exactly this shape, a bare space-delimited name list with no cause field, at `tools/test/run-tests.sh:173`.** **9. DEJAGNU: THE BUG LINK IS DOCUMENTARY BY ITS OWN MANUAL, XPASS IS EXIT 1, AND POSIX 1003.3 DECLINES THE CONCEPT.** The procedure takes a target triplet and an optional bug identifier, and the manual disposes of the second in one clause, DEJAGNU:3058 "argument is optional, and used only in the logging file output; use it" DEJAGNU:3059 "as a link to a bug-tracking system such as GNATS." **That sentence is the survey's authoritative statement that a cause field can be pure documentation, written by the project that ships the field.** The variable exists and is cleared per test, DEJARUNTEST:51 "set xfail_prms	0		;# GNATS prms id number for this expected failure". The triplet is the load-bearing half, and the outcome definition ties the expectation to a bug already known, DEJAGNU:535 "change in a known bug.  If a test fails because the operating system" lacks some facility required by the test, the outcome is `UNSUPPORTED` instead. **Strictness is in the exit code with no switch.** DEJAGNU:496 "if all tests passed including expected failures and unsupported tests." returns 0, and DEJAGNU:498 "if any test failed, passed unexpectedly, or was unresolved." returns 1. **The manual also records one standard that declines the whole concept.** DEJAGNU:348 "POSIX 1003.3 does not incorporate the notion of expected failures, so" `PASS` must be returned for cases expected to fail that did not, and conformance costs the mechanism, DEJAGNU:421 "must avoid the @emph{setup_xfail} procedure as described in the" `PASS` section. **This is DejaGnu's characterization and no POSIX document is pinned here, so it is reported as that project's statement about the standard.** **10. THE GDB TESTSUITE: THE ONE SURVEYED FORMALIZATION OF STATED AGAINST UNDECIDED.** GDB splits expected failure into two result codes by the kind of cause, and ties the identifier requirement to the split. For the project's own defect the bug number is mandatory, GDBTESTREADME:761 "Use KFAIL for known problem of GDB itself.  You must specify the GDB" GDBTESTREADME:762 "bug report number, as in these sample tests:". For an environmental fact it is requested when one exists, GDBTESTREADME:774 ", this indicates a known problem with the" GDBTESTREADME:775 "environment.  This could include limitations of the operating system," compiler version and other components, with GDBTESTREADME:787 "You should provide bug report number for the failing component of the" GDBTESTREADME:788 "environment, if such bug report is available, as with this example" referring to a GCC problem. **A MUST on one arm and a SHOULD-if-available on the other is the distinction the W6 design wants, and GDB draws it by the kind of cause instead of by whether a cause was written down.** Nothing in the survey acts on the split, and DejaGnu's own summary tool folds the two together, DEJAGNU:1170 "columns, but known failures (@samp{KFAIL} and @samp{KPASS}) are" summarized together with expected failures in two additional columns. **GDB then states a rule for when an expectation is the wrong mechanism, and that rule bears on the fork directly.** GDBTESTREADME:797 "Note that it is also acceptable, and often preferable, to avoid" GDBTESTREADME:798 "running the test at all.  This is the better option if the limitation" GDBTESTREADME:799 "is intrinsic to the environment, rather than a bug expected to be" fixed in the near future. **So the surveyed record's one rule about choosing between a skip and an expectation turns on the permanence of the cause, and it lines up with `docs/decisions/decision-scope.md:80-87`: an intrinsic material fact, which is E63's absent CHERI hardware, earns the skip, and a defect expected to close earns the expectation.** **11. RUST AND GO: THE TWO TOOLCHAINS WITH NO EXPECTED-FAILURE CONCEPT.** Rust offers a skip with a documentary reason, RUSTTESTATTR:99 "The [MetaNameValueStr] form of the `ignore` attribute provides a way to specify a reason why the test is ignored." and RUSTTESTATTR:123 "Ignored tests are still compiled when in test mode, but they are not executed." Its cause-carrying attribute belongs to inverted assertion, RUSTTESTATTR:181 "When the [MetaNameValueStr] form or the [MetaListNameValueStr] form with the `expected` key is used, the given string must appear somewhere within the panic message for the test to pass." **That string is load-bearing and it is no kind of expected-failure cause: it asserts what the panic says.** Go has the narrowest offering in the survey. The string `xfail` occurs zero times in `src/testing/testing.go`, measured over the 3,009 pinned lines, and the reason on a skip is a log line, GOTESTING:1376 "// Skip is equivalent to Log followed by SkipNow." **Go also refuses to let a skip launder a failure already recorded**, GOTESTING:1392 "// If a test fails (see Error, Errorf, Fail) and is then skipped," GOTESTING:1393 "// it is still considered to have failed." **12. WHAT AN UNEXPECTED PASS DOES, PER SYSTEM.** *Fails the run always*: LLVM lit (LITCMD:512), DejaGnu (DEJAGNU:498), Chromium by exact outcome set (CHROMEXPDOC:306), and CTest, Jest and Rust `should_panic` by construction. *Fails the run by default with a documented opt-out*: `wptrunner` (WPTRUNNER:407, WPTCLI:81). *Fails the run only when asked*: pytest (PYTESTSKIP:322, :331). *Collected beside failures and rerun by `make recheck`*: Automake (AUTOMAKE:10057, AUTOMAKE:10232). *Counts as a pass*: kselftest (KSELFTESTH:402). *Reporting is optional and treating it as a failure is forbidden*: TAP 14 (TAP14:484, TAP14:488). **13. WHETHER ANY PROJECT STATES A RULE ON PROPORTION.** **No surveyed harness states any rule or guidance about what fraction of a suite may be expected-fail.** Fourteen projects were read for it and none states one. What the record offers instead is descriptive scale, and scale is not a rule: Chromium's `TestExpectations` is 9,205 lines holding 6,793 expectation lines, and `NeverFixTests` is 1,867 lines holding 1,539. Those two are the largest expectation corpus in the survey and the rules they state are about mechanism choice (CHROMTESTEXP:9), bug identifiers (CHROMEXPDOC:277) and duplicates (CHROMEXPDOC:369). **14. WHETHER STRICTNESS IS TREATED AS SUFFICIENT ON ITS OWN.** **By the projects that have it, no. Every one of them adds a second control, and the additions fall into two kinds.** *The tolerance is narrowed on the failure side, so an expectation cannot absorb an arbitrary failure mode*: lit converts only `FAIL` and leaves `TIMEOUT` and `UNRESOLVED` red (LITTESTPY:312), pytest's `raises` refuses an off-list exception (PYTESTSKIP:301), CTest exempts the timeout, CTESTWILLFAIL:7 "still fail regardless of ``WILL_FAIL``." and Automake exempts skips and hard errors (AUTOMAKE:9786). *A second discipline sits beside the tripwire*: Chromium requires an identifier and lints for it and sorts by kind of cause across three files, GDB requires a bug number on the `KFAIL` arm, `wptrunner` carries the implementation-status enumeration, and lit ships `--xfail-not` as an admitted way to silence the tripwire (LITCMD:367). **The one project offering strictness with no second control is pytest, and there strictness is off by default.** **15. THE DIRECT BEARING ON THE FORK.** **On proportion the external record says nothing, and that is the answer here in place of a gap in the search.** **On mechanism choice it supports registering the gate over leaving it out, and two projects state the reason in words that match `tools/test/run-tests.sh:186-196`.** CHROMTESTEXP:9-11 forbids a bare skip in the main suppression file so the subjects keep running on bots, and LITGUIDE:684 prefers `XFAIL` over `UNSUPPORTED` because it alerts you when the subject begins to behave correctly. The tree's own `tal-check.sh` precedent at `tools/test/run-tests.sh:356-360` has no surveyed counterpart: no project here keeps a red gate out of the suite to keep the suite green. **On the stated-against-undecided distinction the record supports the distinction and refuses the form the design gives it.** GDB draws it (GDBTESTREADME:761, :787) and Chromium enforces it (CHROMEXPDOC:277), and in both the stated cause is a durable external identifier a tool can look for. In every system where the cause is prose, the project's own documentation says nothing reads it (DEJAGNU:3058, AUTOMAKE:10537, GOTESTING:1376). **The W6 design's five causes are prose in a design artifact, which puts them in the second class.** **The record supports shipping the gate with five of seven subjects expected-fail on three conditions it can name, and each is met or unmet in the tree today.** (i) *Strictness, met.* `tools/test/run-tests.sh:186` prints `NEWPASS` and :187 increments the counter, and :196 folds that counter into the failure total, which is lit's and DejaGnu's arrangement. (ii) *A narrowed tolerance, unmet.* `KNOWN_FAIL` at :173 absorbs any compile failure whatever for a named root, where every surveyed implementation pins the expectation to a failure mode or to a condition. Three of the five W6 subjects fail on the same absent routing row for `env-open`, which is lit's `REQUIRES`-shaped case and a condition a gate could evaluate once. (iii) *A cause a tool can check, unmet.* No field on `KNOWN_FAIL` holds one, and the tree has no analogue of `lint_test_expectations.py`. **GDB's permanence rule is the one place the record speaks to the five subjects one at a time.** `spawn` has zero definitions tree-wide, which reads as the intrinsic case GDBTESTREADME:798 sends to a skip. The three `env-open` subjects and the truncated skip chain read as the defect-expected-to-close case that earns an expectation. **The record therefore endorses no single uniform verdict over the five, and the design treats them as one class.** **16. WHAT THE SOURCES DO NOT SETTLE.** **No surveyed project states a proportion rule, and this row makes no claim about what any of them would call too many.** **No surveyed tool acts on the difference between an expectation whose cause was written down and one whose cause was left blank.** GDB's split is by kind of cause and its enforcement is human review; Chromium's linter checks that an identifier is present, and no source pinned here says it resolves the identifier or checks that the bug is still open. **Whether Chromium's linter fails a build on a missing identifier is unsettled**: the doc says it will complain, 504 of 6,793 live expectation lines carry no accepted bug form, and `lint_test_expectations.py` was neither pinned nor read. **Coverage gaps, named.** This survey did not reach Meson's `should_fail`; the `test()` reference defers it to the `benchmark` keyword table and that file carries no pin here. JUnit, TestNG and CTest's `PASS_REGULAR_EXPRESSION` stayed outside this survey. Mozilla's reftest `fails-if` manifest conditions and the `mochitest` annotations stayed outside it too, and they are the nearest unread relative of the Chromium and `wptrunner` families. The glibc and GCC xfail-list conventions went unread here. **No POSIX document is pinned**, so the 1003.3 position here rests on DejaGnu's statement about it. **This run read nothing in this tree beyond the five spans it cites**, and it did not check whether `prog/demo/` holds the seven entry roots the W6 design says it holds.

LLVM: `LITGUIDE` (`llvm/llvm-project/main/llvm/docs/TestingGuide.md`, 1,120), `LITCMD` (same repo, `llvm/docs/CommandGuide/lit.md`, 772), `LITTESTPY` (same repo, `llvm/utils/lit/lit/Test.py`, 466). TAP: `TAP14` (`TestAnything/testanything.github.io/master/tap-version-14-specification.md`, 1,184). Chromium: `CHROMEXPDOC` (`chromium/chromium/main/docs/testing/web_test_expectations.md`, 381), `CHROMTESTEXP` (same repo, `third_party/blink/web_tests/TestExpectations`, 9,205), `CHROMNEVERFIX` (same repo, `third_party/blink/web_tests/NeverFixTests`, 1,867). web-platform-tests: `WPTMETA` (`web-platform-tests/wpt/master/tools/wptrunner/docs/expectation.rst`, 366), `WPTRUNNER` (same repo, `tools/wptrunner/wptrunner/wptrunner.py`, 636), `WPTCLI` (same repo, `tools/wptrunner/wptrunner/wptcommandline.py`, 845). Automake: `AUTOMAKE` (`autotools-mirror/automake/master/doc/automake.texi`, 14,345). Go: `GOTESTING` (`golang/go/master/src/testing/testing.go`, 3,009). Linux: `KSELFTESTH` (`torvalds/linux/master/tools/testing/selftests/kselftest.h`, 490), `KTAPSPEC` (same repo, `Documentation/dev-tools/ktap.rst`, 314). DejaGnu: `DEJAGNU` (`git.savannah.gnu.org/cgit/dejagnu.git/plain/doc/dejagnu.texi?h=master`, 6,270), `DEJARUNTEST` (same tree, `runtest.exp`, 2,102). GDB: `GDBTESTREADME` (`sourceware.org/git/?p=binutils-gdb.git` blob_plain of `gdb/testsuite/README` at `HEAD`, 845). Jest: `JESTAPI` (`jestjs/jest/main/docs/GlobalAPI.md`, 1,119). CMake: `CTESTWILLFAIL` (`Kitware/CMake/master/Help/prop_test/WILL_FAIL.rst`, 37). `xlat pin` stamps a UTC clock, so each `.meta` reads `pinned: 2026-09-15`. **Every one of the 97 quoted citations above was resolved against the pinned bytes and checked for uniqueness in its source before this row was written, by a script that refuses a span occurring twice and refuses a span whose first occurrence is at a line other than the one cited.** Live tree spans, opened and measured 2026-09-15 at `7e0eb24`: `tools/test/run-tests.sh:173` (`KNOWN_FAIL` as a bare name list, three entries, no cause field), `:186` (the `NEWPASS` message), `:187` (the counter increment), `:196` (the counter folded into the failure total), `:356-360` (the `tal-check.sh` non-registration and its stated reason), `docs/decisions/decision-scope.md:80-82` (the two-row table splitting track deferral from blocking condition), `:84-87` (E63 as the author's worked example). Counts measured against the pins with `grep`: `CHROMTESTEXP` 9,205 total lines, 6,793 lines beginning with neither `#` nor whitespace, 6,369 containing `crbug`, and 504 containing none of `crbug`, `code.google.com` or `Bug(`; `CHROMNEVERFIX` 1,867 total and 1,539 expectation lines; `GOTESTING` zero occurrences of the string `xfail`. **Six external fetches failed and are named.** `llvm/docs/TestingGuide.rst` and `llvm/docs/CommandGuide/lit.rst` both returned HTTP 404 because those documents are Markdown in the current tree, and the `.md` paths were pinned instead. `llvm/docs/DeveloperPolicy.rst` returned HTTP 404, so no LLVM project-level policy statement about `XFAIL` was checked, and that is a hole in the proportion question on the LLVM side. Three DejaGnu manual locations returned HTTP 404 (`gnu.org/software/dejagnu/manual/dejagnu.html`, `mirror/dejagnu` and `gittup/dejagnu` on GitHub) and the savannah `plain` path succeeded only with `?h=master` appended. `git.savannah.gnu.org/cgit/dejagnu.git/plain/lib/framework.exp` returned HTTP 404, so the `xfail_prms` variable is cited from `runtest.exp` and the emitter that consumes it stayed unread. Meson's `docs/yaml/functions/test.yaml` fetched at HTTP 200 and contains no `should_fail` entry, which the file defers to the `benchmark` keyword table, and that table carries no pin here. **`ledger-lint` was measured before and after this row at the figures in the run report, and check AM stayed green.**

- element:  `none`. This row surveys and answers; it opens no roster row, designs nothing, and answers no author call. It hands the author three named conditions the external record attaches to a strict expectation, says which two the tree fails today, and reports the proportion question as one nobody outside has ruled on.

### FD-33 thirteen things stand between a typed cell grid and pixels, four of four surveyed renderers refuse the font's box-drawing glyphs and draw them in code, and no two surveyed systems store cell width the same way

- state:    OPEN
- claim:    The question, aimed at the choice between rendering the existing `Cell` vocabulary graphically and minting a second vocabulary for the pixel surface: what must be added between a character-cell model and a pixel surface, and where does one cell stop being one drawn box once real fonts are involved? **Eleven systems were read at their own sources: kitty, Alacritty, WezTerm, foot, the Linux framebuffer console, Vim's GUI, Neovim's grid protocol, GNU Emacs, UAX #11, UAX #29, and Markus Kuhn's reference `wcwidth`, with POSIX's `wcwidth` page for the return domain. Twenty-eight pins added, every one raw; four earlier pins reused. The answer has three parts. (a) Thirteen items must exist between a typed grid and pixels, and none of them is a span primitive; the two primitives every surveyed renderer converges on are a solid rectangle fill and a rectangular coverage-mask composite against one colour. (b) Ten places break, and the surveyed projects disagree on two of them out loud. (c) One integer of width per cell is insufficient in every surveyed system, and the reason is normative: UAX #11 states that East_Asian_Width requires per-case tailoring before any terminal use, and that actual display width comes from the font. kitty's shipped answer after that finding is to let the client program declare the width.**
- measured: **1. THE ADDED-COST LIST, THIRTEEN ITEMS, EACH ATTRIBUTED.** *(a) Cell metrics derived from font metrics.* kitty makes this an obligation on every font backend, KITTYFONTSH:47 "FontCellMetrics cell_metrics(PyObject *);", and the struct it returns carries seven derived numbers, KITTYDTYPES:327 "unsigned int cell_width, cell_height, baseline, underline_position, underline_thickness, strikethrough_position, strikethrough_thickness;". Failing to derive one is fatal, KITTYFONTSC:590 "Failed to calculate cell width for the specified font". *(b) A shaper.* KITTYFONTSH:13 "#include <hb.h>"; WEZSHAPE:15 "The [HarfBuzz](https://harfbuzz.github.io/) library is used to perform"; FOOTINI:1993 "rendering, it will use _fcft_ (if compiled with _HarfBuzz_". *(c) A rasterizer call parameterised by subpixel mode.* FOOTRENDER:974 "single = fcft_rasterize_char_utf32(font, base, term->font_subpixel);". *(d) A glyph cache whose key is larger than the codepoint.* kitty's key has nine components, one of which is the position of the cell inside a ligature, KITTYGLYCACHE:33 "glyph_index ligature_index,". *(e) An atlas with a packing policy and two failure modes.* ALACATLAS:12 "pub const ATLAS_SIZE: i32 = 1024;", ALACATLAS:65 "/// Texture atlas is full.", ALACATLAS:69 "GlyphTooLarge,". *(f) A fallback face chain.* KITTYFONTSH:63 "PyObject *create_fallback_face(PyObject *base_face, const ListOfChars *lc, bool bold, bool italic, bool emoji_presentation, FONTS_DATA_HANDLE fg);". *(g) A glyphless path for a codepoint the resolved font lacks.* EMACSDISPX:544 "/* True means the actual glyph is not available, draw using `struct". *(h) Procedural drawing of decorations from the metrics, since no font supplies them.* KITTYDECOR:24 "unsigned half = fcm.underline_thickness / 2;" heads 2,903 lines that draw underline, undercurl, strikethrough, cursor shapes and box characters arithmetically. *(i) A hand-written glyph set for box drawing, block elements, braille and powerline.* ALACBUILTIN:1 "//! Hand-rolled drawing of unicode characters that need to fully cover their character area." opens 1,032 lines of it. *(j) A compositing rule with a gamma term and a contrast term.* KITTYOPTS:281 "Control how kitty composites text glyphs onto the background color. The default", KITTYOPTS:293 "The first number is the gamma adjustment, which controls the thickness of dark". *(k) Subpixel antialiasing as extra render passes.* ALACTEXTMOD:46 "SubpixelPass3 = 3,". *(l) A DPI conversion for every thickness and offset.* FOOTINI:1894 "points. This value is converted to pixels using the monitor's DPI,"; KITTYOPTS:239 "There must be four values corresponding to thin, normal, thick, and very thick". *(m) The blit itself, in the two shapes given in part 5.* **2. THE BREAK LIST, TEN PLACES, WITH WHAT EACH PROJECT DOES.** *(B1) A glyph wider than its cell.* The two surveyed answers contradict each other. foot lets it bleed, on by default, FOOTINI:1869 "Boolean. When enabled, glyphs wider than their cell(s) are allowed" and FOOTINI:1870 "to render into one additional neighbouring cell.", and keeps a per-frame list of the cells it dirtied, FOOTRENDER:995 "* Determine cells that will bleed into their right neighbor and remember". kitty refuses the bleed and deforms the glyph, KITTYFAQ:12 "it will either be rescaled to be smaller or truncated (depending on how much", and names the other behaviour, KITTYFAQ:14 "let the character overflow into neighboring cells, which is fine if the". *(B2) Private-use symbols whose Unicode width is one and whose art wants two cells.* kitty reads the FOLLOWING cell to decide, KITTYFAQ:19 "these symbols are wide and should be rendered in two cells. However, since" and KITTYFAQ:22 "these characters are followed by a space or en-space (U+2002) in which case", restated as an option at KITTYOPTS:113 "if the character is followed by one or more spaces, kitty will use those extra". A cell's drawn extent therefore depends on its neighbour's content. *(B3) Box characters must tile with no seam, which the font does not guarantee.* Four of four surveyed renderers take the glyphs away from the font. foot states the four reasons, FOOTINI:242 "Boolean. When disabled, foot generates box/line drawing characters", FOOTINI:248 "- Line- and box characters are guaranteed to span the entire cell,", FOOTINI:251 "- Many fonts lack some, or all, of the line- and box drawing". WezTerm states it as a workaround it resents, WEZBLOCK:13 "Ideally this option wouldn't exist, but it is present to work around a [hinting issue in freetype](https://gitlab.freedesktop.org/freetype/freetype/-/issues/761).". Alacritty's changelog is a five-year defect list of exactly this class: ALACCHANGES:225 "- Powerline glyphs being cut for narrow fonts", ALACCHANGES:385 "- Quadrants not aligned with half blocks with built-in font", ALACCHANGES:452 "- Bottom gap for certain builtin box drawing characters", ALACCHANGES:489 "- Builtin font lines not contiguous when `font.offset` is used". *(B4) A ligature is one drawn shape over several cells.* kitty keeps the cell by rasterizing the whole run onto a canvas `num_cells` wide and slicing it into one sprite per cell, KITTYFONTSC:1315 "sp[i] = is_repeat_sprite ? sp[i - 1] : sprite_position_for(fg, rf, glyphs, glyph_count, ligature_index++, num_cells);". WezTerm leaves them to the shaper and exposes the OpenType switches. Alacritty's text renderer has no shaper module, ALACTEXTMOD:12 "mod builtin_font;" and ALACTEXTMOD:15 "pub mod glyph_cache;" are its cache and drawing modules and the pinned files contain zero occurrences of `harfbuzz` or `shape` (measured over ALACTEXTMOD, ALACATLAS and ALACCELL). *(B5) A cell holds more than one codepoint.* Three surveyed answers, three caps, none stated with a reason. Alacritty: nine, ALACCELL:17 "const MAX_ZEROWIDTH_CHARS: usize = 9;", held in a side allocation, ALACCELL:133 "zerowidth: ArrayVec<char, MAX_ZEROWIDTH_CHARS>,". kitty: twenty-four, KITTYLINEH:31 "#define MAX_NUM_CODEPOINTS_PER_CELL 24u", reached by flipping the cell's char field into an index. WezTerm: a grapheme string, inline to seven bytes and heap beyond, WEZCELL:727 "text: TeenyString,", WEZCELL:562 "struct TeenyStringHeap {". *(B6) The width of a grapheme cluster is not the sum of its codepoints' widths.* UAX #29 extends clusters to include marks that advance, UAX29:727 "continuing characters are extended to include all spacing combining". foot ships three methods and states that the more correct one breaks callers, FOOTINI:2020 "applications since applications typically use *wcswidth*(3)", FOOTINI:2021 "internally to calculate the width. This results in cursor", with FOOTINI:2024 "*max* uses the width of the largest codepoint in the cluster." as a third. *(B7) The cluster boundary is not the drawn boundary.* UAX29:818 "is not a grapheme cluster. Default grapheme clusters do not" continues "necessarily reflect text display", and UAX29:538 "<p>Grapheme clusters can only provide an" heads the paragraph saying the same for cursor placement. *(B8) Bold and italic faces of a monospaced family are not the same width as the regular face.* KITTYFAQ:32 "faces that have characters wider than the width of the normal face, these", which clips. *(B9) Wide characters need their own face.* Vim's GUI takes a second font for them and states the failure, VIMGUIDOC:1187 "Note: The size of these fonts must be exactly twice as wide as the one", continuing "specified with 'guifont' and the same height. If there is a mismatch then the text will not be drawn correctly." *(B10) Pixel-addressed content cannot be said in cells at all.* kitty's graphics protocol says so as a design goal, KITTYGRAPH:10 "* Should allow specifying graphics to be drawn at individual pixel positions.", adds sub-cell origin offsets, KITTYGRAPH:536 "a different origin within the cell. Note that the offsets must be smaller than the size of the cell.", and a stacking order that puts images under the text, KITTYGRAPH:557 "Negative z-index values mean that the images will be drawn under the text.". **3. WHAT A BITMAP FONT REMOVES, AND WHAT SURVIVES IT.** The Linux framebuffer console is the measured floor: a fixed bitmap font, a 1-bit image per run of cells, and one blit. It REMOVES (b), (c), (f), (j) and (k) from the cost list, and it removes hinting and subpixel positioning with them, FBBITBLIT:154 "u32 width = DIV_ROUND_UP(vc->vc_font.width, 8);" for a one-bit-per-pixel glyph row, with a foreground and a background colour and nothing else, batched a run of cells at a time, FBBITBLIT:187 "image.width = vc->vc_font.width * cnt;". What SURVIVES: (a) still holds, with the metrics now taken from the font's own grid, FBBITBLIT:165 "image.dx = xx * vc->vc_font.width;"; (i) survives untouched, since a bitmap font either has the box characters at the exact cell size or it does not; (l) survives as the choice of which compiled-in size to use, FBCONRST:87 "compiled-in fonts: 10x18, 6x10, 6x8, 7x14, Acorn8x8, MINI4x6,". It ADDS two costs the scalable path does not have. A repertoire cap: the kernel refuses any count except two, FBCONSRC:2491 "if (charcount != 256 && charcount != 512)", and its own comment says the cap is the problem, FBCONSRC:2476 "*  but lets not assume that, since charcount of 512 is small for unicode support.". And a bit-alignment split, because a glyph row narrower than a whole number of bytes needs a second code path, FBBITBLIT:211 "bit_putcs_unaligned(vc, info, s, attribute, cnt,", which FBCONRST:90 "Note, not all drivers can handle font with widths not divisible by 8," says some drivers never got. The width problem, combining marks and exact box tiling all survive the cheapest option. **4. ONE INTEGER OF WIDTH IS INSUFFICIENT IN EVERY SURVEYED SYSTEM.** Six storage answers, no two alike. *chirality*: `(width I64)` at `lib/protocol/grid.chiral:18`. *Alacritty*: no width field; three flags, ALACCELL:30 "const WIDE_CHAR_SPACER          = 0b0000_0000_0100_0000;" and ALACCELL:35 "const LEADING_WIDE_CHAR_SPACER  = 0b0000_0100_0000_0000;", over one `char`, ALACCELL:142 "pub c: char,". *kitty*: a three-bit width, KITTYLINEH:70 "char_type width : WIDTH_BITS;", beside KITTYLINEH:63 "char_type is_multicell : 1;" and a scale-plus-subscale pair, KITTYLINEH:66 "char_type subscale_n : SUBSCALE_BITS;", with the cell's position inside the multicell block and two alignment fields. *WezTerm*: a `usize` beside the bytes, WEZCELL:621 "let width = width.unwrap_or_else(|| grapheme_column_width(s, unicode_version));", with widths one and two stolen into two marker bits of the inline word. *Neovim's grid protocol*: no width channel; a text string per cell and a convention, NVIMUI:376 "The right cell of a double-width char will be represented as the empty", NVIMUI:377 "string. Double-width chars never use `repeat`.". *Emacs*: pixels per glyph, EMACSDISPX:503 "short pixel_width;" and EMACSDISPX:506 "short ascent, descent;", plus a padding flag for the trailing glyphs of a wide character, EMACSDISPX:534 "more than one glyph (e.g. Asian characters).  All but the first". The value is also not derivable. POSIXWCW:75 "positions to be occupied by the wide-character code <i>wc</i>, or return -1 (if <i>wc</i> does not correspond to a printable" gives a fourth return. Kuhn's reference implementation calls itself KUHNWCWIDTH:16 "a first attempt of defining such behavior based on simple rules", says of the Ambiguous class KUHNWCWIDTH:24 "class, the width choice depends purely on a preference of backward" compatibility, and of the Neutral class that the question KUHNWCWIDTH:44 "represented by double-width glyphs cannot yet be answered by" a simple rule. UAX #11 is explicit: UAX11:140 "The East_Asian_Width property is not intended for use by modern terminal emulators without appropriate tailoring on a case-by-case basis.", and UAX11:157 "display width of a glyph is given by the font and may be further adjusted by layout", over UAX11:149 "one of six values" that resolve to two by context. WezTerm's classification therefore has nine variants, one of which is version-dependent, WEZWCW:41 "WidenedIn9," resolved by WEZWCW:1577 "pub fn width_unicode_9_or_later(self) -> u8 {", so the answer depends on which Unicode version the APPLICATION believes. kitty's conclusion is to remove the decision from the terminal: KITTYTEXTSZ:14 "problem of robustly determining the width (in cells) a character should have." and KITTYTEXTSZ:45 "standing terminal ecosystem bugs caused by the client program not knowing how" many cells the terminal will use. `E177` is scoped for a table; the record says a table alone leaves an unresolvable class, and that the field needs a tailoring stance plus a Unicode-version parameter. **5. A GLYPH ATLAS PLUS RECTANGULAR COPIES DOES SUFFICE, AND R3 IS NARROWER THAN STATED.** No surveyed renderer performs a general varying-span write. Two primitives cover every surveyed path. *Fill a rectangle with one colour*: FOOTRENDER:1032 is one of thirteen `pixman_image_fill_rectangles` calls in foot's renderer, used for backgrounds, decorations and the cursor. *Composite a rectangle of coverage against one colour*: foot, which is CPU-only, passes the glyph as the MASK and a solid colour as the source, FOOTRENDER:1080 "PIXMAN_OP_OVER, clr_pix, glyph->pix, pix, 0, 0, 0, 0,". kitty exposes the identical signature for its own CPU path, KITTYFONTSH:73 "const uint8_t *alpha_mask, pixel *dest, const Region *src_rect, const Region *dest_rect, size_t src_stride, size_t dest_stride, pixel color_rgb". Only a colour glyph is a plain image copy. The GPU path replaces the composite with a texture sample over the same rectangle: Alacritty uploads each glyph into the 1024-square atlas and draws quads, ALACATLAS:12; kitty carries one 32-bit sprite index per cell, KITTYLINEH:39 "sprite_index sprite_idx;", and gets ligatures by slicing as in B4. So the requirement on `display-calculus/R3` for this purpose is two primitives over a rectangle, and neither is "varying content in linear time". **Inference, marked**: with a 1-bit bitmap font the pattern already in `prog/demo/sprites.chiral:58-63` expresses the second primitive, since `bcat` of `brepeat` costs one concatenation per RUN per scanline and a 1-bit glyph row is a handful of runs. The worst case for that pattern is a real character rather than a hypothetical: FOOTINI:1901 "MEDIUM SHADE and DARK SHADE) are rendered as solid blocks using a" exists because FOOTINI:1904 "When disabled, they are instead rendered as checker box pattern,", where runs equal pixels. Antialiasing makes every cell that worst case, which is the boundary between the bitmap floor `docs/arcs/native-window-arc.md:44` permits and anything above it. **6. THE BEARING.** The record supports rendering the existing cell vocabulary graphically, with one carve-out. Five shipped cell-faithful graphical renderers were read (kitty, Alacritty, WezTerm, foot, Vim's GUI) plus one grid protocol consumed by graphical front ends (Neovim), and none of them introduced a second TEXT model; every one keeps one grid and hangs a presentation layer off it. Vim states the price as a constraint on the input, VIMGUIDOC:1130 "Note that the fonts must be mono-spaced (all characters have the same width).". The one surveyed system that abandoned cell-faithfulness pays twice: Emacs carries pixels per glyph, EMACSDISPX:503, AND a parallel column notion that has to be reconciled against it, EMACSDISPLAY:5602 "measured in units of the canonical character width (@pxref{Frame" heading a paragraph that exists to make `:align-to` agree with `current-column`. The carve-out is B10: every surveyed system that draws pixel-addressed objects added a SEPARATE vocabulary for them, addressed in pixels, stacked against the text by z-order, and left the cell alone. The supported shape is therefore one cell vocabulary rendered graphically, plus a distinct pixel-addressed object vocabulary if and when images are wanted.  **WHAT THE RECORD DID NOT SETTLE.** (i) Whether a cell should hold a grapheme or a codepoint plus a capped mark list. Three projects, three answers, no stated reason for any cap, and UAX29:818 removes the appeal to segmentation by saying default clusters do not necessarily reflect display. (ii) How many cells a glyph may overflow. foot says one and kitty says zero, and neither cites the other. (iii) Whether any shipped cell-faithful renderer uses a general varying-span write. None of the eleven surveyed does; the negative reaches only as far as the survey. (iv) Alacritty's position on ligatures is not stated in its own pinned docs. `README.md` and `docs/features.md` were read and neither mentions them; the refusal is inferable from the absent shaper module and stops short of a quotable claim. (v) st's FAQ came back wrapped in cgit's HTML source view, went unpinned, and contributes nothing here.

Alacritty: `ALACCELL` (`raw.githubusercontent.com/alacritty/alacritty/master/alacritty_terminal/src/term/cell.rs`, 331), `ALACATLAS` (`alacritty/src/renderer/text/atlas.rs`, 304), `ALACBUILTIN` (`alacritty/src/renderer/text/builtin_font.rs`, 1,032), `ALACTEXTMOD` (`alacritty/src/renderer/text/mod.rs`, 221), `ALACCHANGES` (`CHANGELOG.md`, 1,459). Live tree spans: `lib/protocol/grid.chiral:18`, `prog/demo/sprites.chiral:58-63`, `lib/protocol/render.chiral:424-449`, `docs/arcs/native-window-arc.md:44`, `docs/arcs/display-calculus-arc.md:134`, `docs/elements/catalog.md:495`.
- checked:  2026-09-15
- element:  `none`. This row surveys and prices; it opens no roster row, designs nothing, and answers no author call. It hands the author three things. A thirteen-item cost list with a named floor, since `docs/arcs/native-window-arc.md:44` already permits a bitmap font and the record says which five costs that floor removes and which two it adds. A narrower requirement on `display-calculus/R3`, two rectangle primitives instead of a general varying-span write. And a width finding that reaches `E177`: the field needs a tailoring stance and a Unicode-version parameter, and the one shipped resolution of the class is to let the client declare the width.
